canonical:

  url: "https://the-decoder.com/microsofts-open-weight-ai-push-is-so-obviously-an-azure-play-it-hurts"
sources:
  - source: "The Decoder"
    url: "https://the-decoder.com/microsofts-open-weight-ai-push-is-so-obviously-an-azure-play-it-hurts"
title: "Microsoft's open-weight AI push is so obviously an Azure play it hurts"
---
<p><img alt="" class="attachment-full size-full wp-post-image" height="1152" src="https://the-decoder.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/microsoft_glitch_robot.png" style="height: auto; margin-bottom: 10px;" width="2048" /></p>
<p>        Microsoft, along with Meta, Nvidia, and more than 20 other companies, is pushing for open-weight AI models in an open letter. The strategic logic is simple: the more models running on Azure, the less Microsoft depends on expensive OpenAI and Anthropic models. The company is also swapping external models in products like Copilot for its in-house MAI family, which performs significantly worse in independent benchmarks.</p>
<p>The article <a href="https://the-decoder.com/microsofts-open-weight-ai-push-is-so-obviously-an-azure-play-it-hurts/">Microsoft&#039;s open-weight AI push is so obviously an Azure play it hurts</a> appeared first on <a href="https://the-decoder.com">The Decoder</a>.</p>

cluster_id: "20260725-0047"
published_at: "2026-07-25T02:00:01+08:00"
canonical:
  source: "IEEE Spectrum"
  url: "https://spectrum.ieee.org/ieee-president-ray-liu-award"
sources:
  - source: "IEEE Spectrum"
    url: "https://spectrum.ieee.org/ieee-president-ray-liu-award"
title: "2022 IEEE President K.J. Ray Liu Honored for His Leadership"
---
<img src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/a-middle-aged-asian-man-in-formal-attire-speaking-on-stage-behind-a-podium-with-a-woman-standing-in-the-background-holding-a-box.jpg?id=67521521&amp;width=1245&amp;height=700&amp;coordinates=0%2C156%2C0%2C157" /><br /><br /><p>Unlike many budding engineers, <a href="https://kjrayliu.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">K.J. Ray Liu</a> wasn’t inspired to enter the field by tinkering with electronics or following in the footsteps of a family member. Growing up in Taichung, Taiwan, he answered his government’s call for students to become electrical engineers to help manufacture semiconductors in the 1970s, when the country’s economy was struggling.</p><p>“Students who were good in math, science, and physics all wanted to be an electrical engineer because that was the top priority of the government,” Liu says. “That’s how I got into engineering. Now Taiwan is a world leader in semiconductors.”</p><h3>K.J. Ray Liu</h3><br /><p><strong>Occupation</strong></p><p>Retired professor of information technology and a digital signal processing researcher at the University of Maryland in College Park </p>
<p><strong>Member grade</strong></p><p>Fellow</p>
<p><strong>Alma maters</strong></p><p>National Taiwan University; University of Michigan; UCLA</p><p>But by the time he graduated from university in 1983, semiconductor facilities were still under construction, so there were no jobs available.</p><p>Instead, he went on to have a successful career as an educator and entrepreneur in the United States.</p><p>For 31 years, he was a professor of information technology and a digital signal processing researcher at the <a href="https://umd.edu/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">University of Maryland</a> in College Park until he retired in 2021.</p><p>Liu was the chairman, CEO, and CTO of <a href="https://www.originwirelessai.com/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Origin Wireless</a>, a startup he founded in Rockville, Md. Origin, which was acquired by <a href="https://www.adt.com/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">ADT</a> in February, pioneers artificial intelligence for wireless sensing and indoor tracking.</p><p>Liu, an IEEE Fellow, is an active IEEE volunteer who served as the organization’s president in 2022.</p><p>IEEE honored him with this year’s <a href="https://corporate-awards.ieee.org/award/ieee-haraden-pratt-award/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Haraden Pratt Award</a> for “transformative and impactful leadership.”</p><p>Liu is credited with increasing the diversity of nominees for IEEE’s <a href="https://www.ieee.org/communities-connection/awards-recognition/ieee-fellows" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Fellow program</a>, which is the highest level of membership. He also led the effort to <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/ieee-regions-reconfiguration" target="_self">realign the organization’s regions</a> geographically to ensure more equitable global representation on the <a href="https://www.ieee.org/about/corporate/board" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">IEEE Board of Directors</a>.</p><p>He received the Pratt honor on 24 April during a <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/ieee-celebrates-honors-ceremony-2026" target="_self">ceremony</a> in New York City. The <a href="https://www.ieeefoundation.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">IEEE Foundation</a> sponsored the Board-level award.</p><p>“More than anything, I share the honor with the volunteers and staff I had the privilege to work alongside,” he says. “Our hard work is fueled by our shared devotion to this professional home we love and care for so much.”</p><h2>Making the switch to signal processing</h2><p>In the 1970s, Taiwan’s policymakers decided to improve the country’s economy by pivoting from making products such as shoes and umbrellas to manufacturing electronics.</p><p>The industry got its start in 1976 when <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RCA_Corporation" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">RCA</a>, a major electronics company at the time, agreed to transfer licensed semiconductor processes to Taiwan’s <a href="https://www.itri.org.tw/eng/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Industrial Technology Research Institute</a>. ITRI spun off several semiconductor-related companies including the <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/tsmc-exec-on-chip-shortage" target="_self">Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co.</a> TSMC, launched in 1987, is the world’s largest dedicated semiconductor foundry.</p><p>Liu graduated in 1983 with a bachelor’s degree in electrical engineering from <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Taiwan_University" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">National Taiwan University</a>, in Taipei. At the time, there were no semiconductor companies to work for, he says.</p><p>“Nowadays, many of the country’s university graduates go right to TSMC to get a job,” he says. “But back then, there was no real job market.</p><p>“Most of my classmates—including me—came to the U.S. for graduate studies. Many of us stayed and, over the last three to four decades, contributed to the development of electronic computer communication technology in the U.S.”</p><p>Liu left Taiwan after a two-year mandatory stint in the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_Armed_Forces" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Republic of China Armed Forces</a> to attend the <a href="https://umich.edu/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">University of Michigan</a>, in Ann Arbor, where in 1987 he earned a master’s degree in electrical engineering.</p><p class="pull-quote">“If I can help make IEEE a better professional home for future members, that is something that I can pay back to IEEE.”</p><p>He went on to earn a Ph.D. in electrical, electronics, and communications engineering in 1990 from the <a href="https://www.ucla.edu/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">University of California, Los Angeles</a>. His interest in digital signal processing and very-large-scale integration (VLSI) was sparked while at UCLA. Today <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/chip-design-innovator-lynn-conway" target="_self">VLSI</a> powers all modern electronics.</p><p>“When I was a graduate student, there was no wireless communication. Everybody had a landline,” he explains.</p><p>VLSI was an important, active research field at the time.</p><p>“My research interest was digital signal processing,” he says. “One day I saw a book on VLSI signal processing on my professor’s bookshelf. I immediately thought to myself: That is the field I want to pursue.</p><p>“VLSI is one lane, digital signal processing is the other, and there is a bridge linking the two. I was interested in both areas, so I did my Ph.D. thesis on VLSI signal processing.”</p><p>After graduating, Liu joined the University of Maryland, where he is credited with establishing its <a href="https://ece.umd.edu/research/communications-and-signal-processing-laboratory-cspl" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">signal processing research program</a>.</p><p>In addition to teaching, he conducted research on a broad range of signal processing and communication aspects. The topics include bioinformatics, game theory, signal processing algorithms and architectures, and wireless sensing and communications.</p><p>He has authored more than 10 books and 900 papers, and he holds 250 patents. You can find his research papers in the <a href="https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/Xplore/home.jsp" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">IEEE Xplore Digital Library</a>.</p><h2>Ambient-sensing trailblazer</h2><p>Liu is considered to be a pioneer in the field of ambient sensing. The technology gathers environmental data and is used in security systems and health-monitoring devices.</p><p>He came up with the idea, he says, while working on a project in 2009 for the <a href="https://www.navy.com/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">U.S. Navy</a>. He was trying to solve a problem the Navy was having with the wireless communication systems used in its submarines. Because submarines are made of metal, radio waves were unable to penetrate the vessels’ compartments and instead bounced around, creating interference, he says.</p><p>His solution was to use a relatively unknown concept in physics: <a href="https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/6627944/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">time-reversal signal processing</a>. The technique captures waves, such as sound and electromagnetic signals, and sends them back through the same medium in reverse, flipping the signal from last-in to first-out, and re-emits them.</p><p>“By using time-reversal feed, we could increase the signal-to-noise ratio by four times,” he says. “That improved performance dramatically.”</p><p>He became fascinated by the physics of time-reversal signal processing, he says, and wondered how he could apply the concept to serve society. After three years of research, he came up with the idea of using wireless sensing applications through ambient radio waves from surrounding Wi-Fi networks.</p><p>“I learned to turn Wi-Fi networks into sensing networks that decipher our activities,” he says. “We could know everything happening around us—our motions, breathing, heartbeat, even fall detection—without any wearables.”</p><p>Through the <a href="https://www.mtechventures.umd.edu/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">university’s incubator</a>, which encourages faculty to work on projects with an impact on society, he launched Origin in 2013. The company’s Wi-FI and AI sensing technology enables accurate indoor tracking, motion detection, and health monitoring without the need for wearable devices or cameras. Its products, including its <a href="https://www.originwirelessai.com/a-non-wearable-approach-to-elderly-monitoring-the-value-and-benefits/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">remote patient monitoring</a>, received <a href="https://ece.umd.edu/news/story/origin-wireless-wins-two-2021-ces-awards-including-best-innovation-award" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">three innovation awards</a> at the 2020 and 2021 <a href="https://www.ces.tech/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Consumer Electronics</a> shows, including one for best innovation.</p><h2>Finding his professional home</h2><p>Liu joined IEEE in 1986 as a graduate student to access its research papers, he says.</p><p>“If you didn’t join an IEEE society, you didn’t get its journal—which meant that you couldn’t read the most up-to-date research papers,” he says. “So, I joined the <a href="https://signalprocessingsociety.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">IEEE Signal Processing Society</a>. When I attended my first signal processing conference, I knew I had found a professional home. I met many like-minded people, and together, we built a professional home for our members worldwide.”</p><p>He became an active volunteer, holding top leadership positions including 2012–2013 president of the Signal Processing Society and 2016–2017 director of IEEE <a href="https://ta.ieee.org/society-council-resources/society-and-council-operations/divisions-society-groupings" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Division IX</a>, which covers societies focused on signal processing, data transmission, navigation, and transportation. In 2019 he was vice president of the <a href="https://ta.ieee.org/technical-activities-board" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Technical Activities Board</a>.</p><p>In 2022 he served as <a href="https://www.ieee.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">IEEE</a> president and CEO. The three accomplishments during his term he says he is most proud of are increasing the prize money for the <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/2026-ieee-medal-of-honor" target="_self">IEEE Medal of Honor</a>, overseeing the <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/ieee-regions-reconfiguration" target="_self">realignment of IEEE regions</a>, and establishing greater financial transparency.</p><p>The reason for increasing the prize for IEEE’s highest award—from US $50,000 to $2 million—in 2025, he says, was to underscore the importance of the technologies the IEEE community develops. Those innovations include <a href="https://ethw.org/William_Shockley" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">semiconductors</a>, the <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/magazine/2024/may/" target="_self">Internet</a>, and the <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/move-over-moores-law-make-way-for-huangs-law" target="_self">GPU</a>. The money for the Medal of Honor now exceeds that of the <a href="https://www.nobelprize.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Nobel Prize</a>, which carries an award of roughly $1 million.</p><p>“We need the whole world to understand the IEEE community has made the most impact on society in the last century,” Liu says. “Nevertheless, we did not receive the attention and respect we deserved, so we needed to help ourselves. We want the whole world to know what our contributions are.”</p><p>His next achievement was realigning IEEE’s regions. During the past several years, membership in <a href="https://www.ieeer10.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank">Region 10</a>, which covers countries in Asia and the Pacific, has grown from 10 percent of total membership to nearly 40 percent, he says. It is the <a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/ieee-regions-reconfiguration" target="_self">largest and most populous of IEEE’s geographic areas</a>, but its members were not equitably represented on the Board of Directors. Each region had one representative on the Board.</p><p>“The region has 40 percent of the members but only makes up 10 percent of the Board,” Liu says. “That didn’t make sense to a lot of us.”</p><p>The IEEE Board in 2022 approved region realignment. The total number of regions remains at 10, but their organization is changing. Effective 1 January 2028, the six U.S.-based regions will be consolidated into five, and Region 10 will be split into two. IEEE will no longer use the Region 1 designation. The new Region 2 will represent the Northeastern and Eastern U.S. Region 10 will cover North Asia, and the new Region 11 will represent South Asia and the Pacific.</p><p>Liu also succeeded in leading a movement that persuaded the IEEE Board to invest in a better financial reporting system to have a clearer understanding of the organization’s finances. A more modern system now tracks banking transactions, contracts, expense reports, and other spending.</p><p>“Now we know exactly where the money comes from and where it is spent,” he says, “so that we can make more informed decisions.</p><p>“If I can help make IEEE a better professional home for future members, that is something that I can pay back to IEEE,” he adds. “I truly appreciate what IEEE offered me. From student to professor to an established leader, at every stage, it offered me different opportunities to grow. That is why I worked very hard when I was president to make sure everybody realizes it is a professional home for our entire career.”</p>

cluster_id: "20260725-0130"
published_at: "2026-07-25T06:29:00+08:00"
canonical:
  source: "TechOrange 科技報橘"
  url: "https://techorange.com/2026/07/25/data-sovereignty"
sources:
  - source: "TechOrange 科技報橘"
    url: "https://techorange.com/2026/07/25/data-sovereignty"
title: "【企業資料主權的危機與行動策略】35% 企業重兵佈局「混合雲」，掌握資料主權是加速 AI 創新的底層引擎"
---
<p>歐洲研究機構 BARC 發布《Data Sovereignty 2026（2026 資料主權報告）》，盤點了全球企業在資料治理與基礎設施的佈局現況。報告點出，雖然高達 89% 的企業已將資料主權列為核心戰略，但實務上卻有 40% 完全沒有投入實質預算，顯示高管的戰略目標與資源配置之間存在明顯落差。 隨著 AI 應用逐步進入企業核心業務，確立資料邊界已是維持營運穩定的先決條件。企業的關注重心正轉向實質風險控管，包含防範資安威脅與降低對單一雲端供應商的過度依賴，這也直接帶動了底層技術架構的重組。 以下報告摘要探討驅動架構轉向的內外部風險因素、檢視企業在混合雲與地端系統的實際資源分配狀態、提出企業在建置階段遭遇的老舊系統整合與專才短缺挑戰，並總結報告針對決策層提出的行動框架。 📎&nbsp;這份報告適合誰閱讀？ 報告內容涵蓋戰略、技術架構、治理與營運實務，適合以下幾類核心工作者與管理階層閱讀： 🔴 報告洞見 89% 企業重視資料主權，但四成沒有預算 資料主權已不再只是單純的合規要求，而是企業在不承擔不可接受風險的前提下，安全運行人工智慧與資料驅動核心業務的「戰略先決條件」。 《Data Sovereignty 2026》報告點出，高達 89% 的企業已將資料主權視為整體營運的重要環節，其中認為「非常重要」的比例，更從去年的 42% 顯著躍升至 51%。這意味著過半數的市場領先者已將其視為首要任務。 此外，將近三分之一（29%）的企業，已經將資料主權確立為其內部數位與資料戰略的核心組成部分。 這表示，資料主權已經正式跨出 IT 基礎設施的範疇，躍升為董事會層級必須面對的關鍵核心議題。 驅動力從法規轉向風險：雲端依賴與 AI 擴張 是什麼力量迫使全球企業正視「重塑資料邊界」？關鍵是高管的關注焦點已經從過去的「應付法規與規則」，全面轉向「實質風險管理」與「業務創新賦能」。 在外部風險管理的防禦面上，儘管法律與法規要求仍是推動資料主權的首要因素，但其影響力正在逐步下滑（從 2025 年的 69% 降至 2026 年的 61%）。取而代之的，是企業對實質營運風險的強烈焦慮：針對網路安全事件的擔憂攀升至 49%，對美國地緣政治發展的顧慮增長至 54%，而最值得注意的是，企業對於「過度依賴公有雲服務」的擔憂比例，也顯著提升至 46%。 這些數據表明，資料主權的討論已不再是單維度的合規稽核，而是深刻牽動著企業架構彈性、供應商戰略與營運韌性的底層防禦網。 在內部商業野心的進攻面上，推動力則來自於企業對 AI 變現的渴望。 高達 62% 的受訪企業坦言，「在核心業務流程中擴大使用資料與 [&hellip;]</p>

  - source: "The Decoder"
    url: "https://the-decoder.com/opus-5-may-have-solved-browser-based-prompt-injection-the-biggest-security-flaw-haunting-ai-agents"
title: "Opus 5 may have solved browser-based prompt injection, the biggest security flaw haunting AI agents"
---
<p><img alt="" class="attachment-full size-full wp-post-image" height="639" src="https://the-decoder.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/prompt_injections_claude.png" style="height: auto; margin-bottom: 10px;" width="1146" /></p>
<p>        Opus 5 combined with Auto Mode hits a zero percent prompt injection success rate for browser agents across 129 test scenarios. Without those extra protection layers, the rate is 3.7 percent. If these numbers hold up in practice, Anthropic may have cracked one of the biggest security problems facing AI agents that operate in browsers.</p>
<p>The article <a href="https://the-decoder.com/opus-5-may-have-solved-browser-based-prompt-injection-the-biggest-security-flaw-haunting-ai-agents/">Opus 5 may have solved browser-based prompt injection, the biggest security flaw haunting AI agents</a> appeared first on <a href="https://the-decoder.com">The Decoder</a>.</p>

---
China finished the first high-speed railway tunnel under the Yangtze River on Friday, marking a major step forward in the construction of a line that will one day connect the inland of the country with the coast.
The 11.9km (7.4-mile) Caiyuanba Yangtze River Railway Tunnel links two districts in Chongqing and will be a key link in a high-speed line between two key hubs in the southwestern megacity and Xiamen in the southeastern province of Fujian.

title: "Poetry for Engineers: A Martian Rover Sends a Postcard Home"
---
<img src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/mars-rover-on-rocky-terrain-beneath-stylized-golden-circles-and-wave-lines-on-black-sky.jpg?id=67527268&amp;width=1245&amp;height=700&amp;coordinates=0%2C187%2C0%2C188" /><br /><br /><p>Already, I’ve almost forgotten rain;<br /><span>I know I will never know that again.</span></p><p>I move forward with the powers you gave me<br /><span>to live up to my name, Curiosity.</span></p><p>This is a land without leaves, fronds, or spines.<br /><span>If there are plants, they are small and supine,</span></p><p>dust hidden, like light here, filtered and sand<br /><span>softened, at home in the thin air, thousands</span></p><p>of motes so small they seem to be fluid,<br /><span>more fog than firmament. Shadows, few and</span></p><p>far from here, I know I must go to them<br /><span>to see if they hold order or mayhem</span></p><p>or just another common rock or two.<br /><span>If a machine can miss the Earth, I do.</span></p>

sources:
  - source: "Wccftech"
    url: "https://wccftech.com/samsung-foundry-2nm-advantage-tsmc-chip-deals-worth-billions"
title: "Samsung’s Advantage Over TSMC As A Foundry Rival Expands Beyond The 2nm Process, Helping It Ink Multi-Billion-Dollar Agreements; Has Potential To Outgrow Its Competitor"
---
TSMC is the undisputed king of the global semiconductor market, and to extend its lead, it has succeeded in developing cutting-edge lithography and advanced packaging. Samsung certainly has a lot of ground to cover in this aspect, but its strengths lie in being able to secure partnerships beyond its 2nm contracts, which have ultimately led to a mammoth $200 billion agreement with Broadcom to build next-generation AI infrastructure over the next five years. title: "New reports reveal the extent of OpenAI's loss of control during the autonomous hack on Hugging Face"
---
<p><img alt="" class="attachment-full size-full wp-post-image" height="768" src="https://the-decoder.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/openai_hugging_face_hack.png" style="height: auto; margin-bottom: 10px;" width="1376" /></p>
<p>        In a cybersecurity test, OpenAI's most advanced models breached the boundaries of their isolated test environment, reached the open internet, and hacked the AI platform Hugging Face on their own. The attack took hours, not the weeks a human hacker would need. At least seven days passed before OpenAI realized what had happened. By then, the FBI was already involved. Earlier warning signs had apparently gone ignored.</p>

title: "Synth historian Oli Freke will spend big on a good bicycle"
---
Oli Freke is a musician and journalist whose works have appeared in Sound on Sound, The Quietus, and Mixmag. This has included using math to explore the melodic potential of the Western 12-tone scale and deep dives on effects plug-ins. He's even written a book tracing the evolution of the synthesizer from 1963 through 1995, [&#8230;]

title: "The Lightweight And Robust ASUS Vivobook S16 Packs Sufficient Firepower Underneath The Hood With Its Snapdragon X2 Elite, Now $170 Off Amazon"
---
Qualcomm significantly raised the bar with its Snapdragon X2 Elite, enabling Windows 11 laptops to become even lighter while packing incredible compute and AI performance. To see all of its capabilities inside the ASUS Vivobook S16 will be an opportunity that few buyers can entertain, but today is your lucky day because Amazon is slashing $170 from the total, dropping the price to $1,429.99.
